The **Society of the Muslim Brothers** ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): جماعة الإخوان المسلمين, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic): *Jamāʿat al-Ikhwān al-Muslimīn*), better known as the **Muslim Brotherhood** (الإخوان المسلمون, *al-Ikhwān al-Muslimūn*), is a transnational [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) [Islamist](15012.md) organization founded in [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Egypt) by [Islamic scholar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_studies), [imam](15240.md) and schoolteacher [Hassan al-Banna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_al-Banna) in 1928.[^cite_note-BorgesonValeri2009-33] Al-Banna's teachings spread far beyond Egypt, influencing various Islamist movements from charitable organizations to political parties.[^cite_note-34]

Initially, as a [pan-Islamist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Islamist), religious, and [social movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_movement), its members preached [Islam in Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Egypt), taught the [illiterate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illiterate), and set up hospitals and business enterprises. The group later advanced into the [political arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_Islam), aiming to end [British colonial rule in Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Egypt_under_the_British). The movement's self-stated aim is the establishment of a state ruled by *[Sharia](28840.md)* under a [caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate),[^cite_note-35] with one of its most well-known slogans being "Islam is the solution".[^cite_note-36] The group combines political activism with Islamic charity work.[^cite_note-BBC_profile-1]

The group spread to other [Muslim countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_countries) but still has one of its largest organizations in Egypt, despite a succession of government crackdowns since 1948.[^cite_note-37] It remained a fringe group in the politics of the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world) until the 1967 [Six-Day War](29328.md), when Islamism replaced popular secular [Arab nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_nationalism) after a resounding Arab defeat by the [State of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Israel).[^cite_note-38] The movement was also supported by [Saudi Arabia](349303.md), with which it shared mutual enemies like [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism).[^cite_note-39]

The [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) initially brought the group legalization and substantial political power, but that expansion had suffered reversals by 2013.[^cite_note-Ibish-40] The Egyptian Muslim Brotherhood was legalized in 2011 and won several elections,[^cite_note-wade-41] including the [2012 Egyptian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Egyptian_presidential_election), in which its candidate, [Mohamed Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Morsi), became Egypt's first democratically elected president.[^cite_note-42] Following [massive demonstrations and unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012%E2%80%932013_Egyptian_protests), Morsi was overthrown in the [2013 Egyptian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Egyptian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) orchestrated by the military and placed under house arrest; a later review found that the Muslim Brotherhood had failed to moderate its views or embrace democratic values during its time in power.[^cite_note-43] The group was then banned in Egypt and declared a terrorist organization. The [Persian Gulf](24761.md) monarchies of [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) and the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) followed suit.[^cite_note-44]

The group's founder accepted the utility of [political violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence),[^cite_note-45] and members of the Brotherhood conducted assassinations and attempted assassinations on Egyptian state figures during his lifetime, including Egyptian prime minister [Mahmoud El Nokrashy Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_El_Nokrashy_Pasha) in 1948.[^cite_note-jabr-46] In his 1964 book *[Milestones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milestones_\(book\))* (معالم في الطريق, *Maʿālim fī aṭ-Ṭarīq*), [Sayyid Qutb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayyid_Qutb), one of the group's most prominent thinkers, promoted [takfirism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takfiri), a doctrine permitting "the stigmatisation of other Muslims as [infidel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infidel) or [apostate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy_in_Islam), and of existing states as un-Islamic, and the use of extreme violence in the pursuit of the perfect Islamic society"; the doctrine continues to inspire many [jihadist movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jihadist_movement).[^cite_note-Moussalli_2012-47][^cite_note-Polk_2018-48] The group abandoned the use of violence in the 1970s. The Muslim Brotherhood since does not have a clear ideological platform on violence.[^cite_note-49]

In recent years, the primary state backers of the Muslim Brotherhood have been [Qatar](8238258.md) and [AKP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Development_Party_\(Turkey\))-ruled [Turkey](11125639.md).[^cite_note-50] As of 2026, the flagship group or specific chapters are considered a terrorist organization by the governments of [Argentina](18951905.md), [Kenya](188171.md), [Germany](11867.md), [Bahrain](18933277.md),[^cite_note-BNA-24] [Egypt](8087628.md),[^cite_note-EgyptGovtDeclaresTerrorist-51] [Russia](25391.md),[^cite_note-base.consultant.ru-52] [Saudi Arabia](349303.md),[^cite_note-53] the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md),[^cite_note-Alaa_Shahine_and_Glen_Carey,_Bloomberg_News-54] and the [United States](3434750.md).[^cite_note-55]

## Classification

In academic research, four different approaches to understanding the Muslim Brotherhood are distinguished. The first approach draws a clear connection between the Muslim Brotherhood and terrorist organizations such as [al‑Qaeda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al%E2%80%91Qaeda). It is argued that the Brotherhood refrains from violence for tactical reasons and merely seeks to gain power. The organization is portrayed as a wolf in sheep's clothing that cooperates with terrorist groups and allegedly shares ideological similarities with them. The second approach overlaps partly with the first. It views the Muslim Brotherhood as an unchanging group for which a strict interpretation of the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) and the [Sunna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah) is essential. This perspective attributes a supposedly undemocratic character to the Brotherhood; the organization is said to strive for an [Islamic theocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_theocracy) in individual countries and ultimately worldwide. The third approach regards the Muslim Brotherhood as an international conspiracy against [the West](21208200.md), pursuing a hidden agenda that it skillfully conceals. Individuals associated with the Brotherhood are described as a "spider in the web" or a "Trojan horse," or compared to tentacles. This imagery suggests that the Muslim Brotherhood functions as a central, controlling power. Organizations linked to it are said to adopt different names in order to obscure their traces. The fourth approach is the most common among scholars who have conducted research on the Muslim Brotherhood. It views the organization as pragmatic, dynamic, and flexible, capable of adapting and accepting political rules of the game. The Brotherhood is seen as exerting pressure on regimes to implement democratic and constitutional reforms and as being receptive to the demands of the population. Proponents of this approach do not claim that the Muslim Brotherhood is a liberal‑democratic organization. However, they identify real, organizational, intensely debated, and ideologically grounded developments within the movement.[^cite_note-56]

## Foundation and history in Egypt

Main article: [Muslim Brotherhood in Egypt](30726095.md)

### Early years

Main article: [Ittihad Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ittihad_Party)

#### Formative period (1928–1936)



The founder of the Muslim Brotherhood, [Hassan al-Banna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_al-Banna)

[Hassan al-Banna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_al-Banna) founded the Muslim Brotherhood in the city of [Ismailia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismailia) in March 1928 along with six workers of the [Suez Canal](29323.md) Company, as a [Pan-Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Islamic), religious, political, and [social movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_movement).[^cite_note-57] They appointed Al-Banna as their leader and vowed to work for [Islam](6037917.md) through *[Jihad](16203.md)* and revive Islamic Brotherhood. Thus, the Muslim Brothers were born; under the pledge that its members would

> be soldiers in the call to Islam, and in that is the life for the country and the honour for the *[Umma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ummah)*... We are brothers in the service of Islam.. Hence we are the "Muslim Brothers".[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-Bellaigue-59]

The Suez Canal Company helped Banna build the mosque in Ismailia that would serve as the Brotherhood's headquarters, according to [Richard P. Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_P._Mitchell)'s *The Society of Muslim Brothers*.[^cite_note-60] According to al-Banna, contemporary Islam had lost its social dominance, because most Muslims had been corrupted by Western influences. Sharia law based on the [Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qur'an) and the [Sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah) were seen as laws passed down by God that should be applied to all parts of life, including the organization of the government and the handling of everyday problems.[^cite_note-61]

Al-Banna was populist in his message of protecting workers against the tyranny of foreign and monopolist companies. It founded social institutions such as hospitals, pharmacies, schools, etc. Al-Banna held highly conservative views on issues such as women's rights, opposing equal rights for women, but supporting the establishment of justice towards women.[^cite_note-Women-62] The Brotherhood grew rapidly going from 800 members in 1936, to 200,000 by 1938 and over 2 million by 1948.[^cite_note-FFPIW-60-63]

#### Entry into politics (1936–1939)

As its influence grew, it opposed British rule in Egypt starting in 1936.[^cite_note-brill-64] al-Banna had been in contact with [Amin al-Husseini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amin_al-Husseini) since 1927.[^cite_note-65] A central concern for the early Muslim Brotherhood was its pro-Arab activism for the Arab-Zionist conflict in Palestine, which in 1936–1939 culminated in the [great Arab revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936%E2%80%931939_Arab_revolt_in_Palestine).[^cite_note-GershoniJankowski_223-224-66] While absent before the outbreak of the revolt,[^cite_note-Berridge_p78-67] the Brotherhood now began to make use of aggressive anti-Jewish rhetorics which also targeted the [Jewish community in Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_in_Egypt).[^cite_note-GershoniJankowski_223-224-66] The official weekly of the Brotherhood, [*al-Nadhir*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Nadhir), published a series of articles titled "The Danger of Jews", warning of alleged Jewish plots against Islam like [Freemasonry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freemasonry) or [Marxism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism).[^cite_note-Mellor_107-68] In 1938 *al-Nadhir* demanded from Egypt's Jews to either adopt an openly anti-Zionist stance or to face "hostility". It also criticized the prominent role of Jews in Egypt's society and their prominence in journalism, commercial spheres and the entertainment industry. *al-Nadhir* even called for a boycott and their expulsion, "for they have corrupted Egypt and its population."[^cite_note-GershoniJankowski_223-224-66] In another instance the Jews were referred to as a "societal cancer".[^cite_note-Berridge_p78-67] The Brotherhood eventually distributed a list of Jewish business owners and called for their boycott,[^cite_note-69] claiming that they supported the Zionists.[^cite_note-70] Such conflations of Jews and Zionists were common.[^cite_note-Mellor_107-68][^cite_note-71]

In the years preceding World War II the Muslim Brothers likely received indirect funds from [Nazi Germany](21212.md), maintained via the [Deutsches Nachrichtenbüro](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsches%20Nachrichtenb%C3%BCro) in Cairo and Amin al-Husseini,[^cite_note-Frampton_51-52-72] who himself received funds from [Italian intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Servizio_Informazioni_Militare)[^cite_note-73] and the [Abwehr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abwehr).[^cite_note-74] Being interested in strengthening a militant anti-British organization, Germany may have funded the Brotherhood as early as 1934. One later British source claimed that in 1936 alone, Germany transferred over £5.000.[^cite_note-Frampton_51-52-72] The author Francis Nicosia found in his research however that the Germans had generally refused to assist Arab independence movements and related activities before 1938 due to a desire to keep positive relations with Britain.[^cite_note-75] al-Banna and other members of the Brotherhood voiced admiration for aspects of [Nazi ideology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism), including its militarism and its centralization [revolving around a charismatic leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolf_Hitler's_cult_of_personality),[^cite_note-76] but opposed others like its racial policies and ethnic nationalism.[^cite_note-77] The outbreak of the war ended any relationship between Germany and the Muslim Brothers.[^cite_note-GershoniJankowski_213-78] al-Banna denied that he had ever received German funding.[^cite_note-79] [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascist_Italy_\(1922%E2%80%931943\)) funding of the Brotherhood is unlikely, as the latter vehemently opposed the [Italian occupation of Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Libya).[^cite_note-GershoniJankowski_213-78]

#### World War II (1939–1945)

| \[icon] | This section **needs expansion**. You can help by [adding missing information](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Muslim_Brotherhood\&action=edit\&section=)[adding missing information](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Muslim_Brotherhood\&action=edit\&section=) or [making an edit request](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Talk%3AMuslim_Brotherhood\&preload=Template%3ASubmit+an+edit+request%2Fpreload\&action=edit\&section=new\&editintro=Template%3AEdit+extended-protected%2Feditintro\&preloadtitle=Extended-confirmed-protected+edit+request+on+2+October+2026\&preloadparams%5B%5D=edit+extended-protected\&preloadparams%5B%5D=Muslim+Brotherhood). *(January 2023)* |
| --- | --- |

During the early part of World War II, the Brotherhood at times displayed pro-Axis sympathies. Worried, the British kept the Brotherhood under firm control by temporarily banning its newsletters, surveiling its meetings and arresting various provincial leaders. al-Banna himself was briefly taken into custody and eventually acknowledged his loyalty to the British, although the latter remained suspicious.[^cite_note-80]

By 1942 the Muslim brotherhood had begun receiving British funding in coordination with the pro-British [Wafd Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wafd_Party).[^cite_note-81] During World War II the Muslim Brotherhood openly condemned the racist and chauvinist tendencies of Nazi Germany and Fascist Italy in its newspapers, something noted by a British intelligence report from 1942 which stated “although the Muslim Brotherhood may have imitated Nazi and fascist organizational forms, they have no particular sympathy for Nazi ideology”.[^cite_note-82]



A gathering of senior youth scouts (*jawala*, lit. "travellers") in the 1940s.

Between 1938 and 1940[^cite_note-elZalaf_22-83] or 1941[^cite_note-Frampton_69-84] the Brotherhood formed an armed wing called the ["Secret Apparatus"](https://ar.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D8%A7%D9%84%D9%86%D8%B8%D8%A7%D9%85%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%AE%D8%A7%D8%B5%20%D8%A8%D8%AC%D9%85%D8%A7%D8%B9%D8%A9%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%A5%D8%AE%D9%88%D8%A7%D9%86%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D9%85%D8%B3%D9%84%D9%85%D9%8A%D9%86) (*al-Nizam al-Khas*),[^cite_note-elZalaf_22-83] also known as "Special Apparatus".[^cite_note-85] This group was a successor[^cite_note-86] of the "battalions" (*kata'ib*) established in late 1937.[^cite_note-87] Its goal was to fight the British until their expulsion from Egypt, British collaborators as well as Zionists.[^cite_note-88] It also protected the Brotherhood against the police and infiltrated the Communist movement.[^cite_note-89] The "Secret Apparatus" was led by a committee of five, with each of them commanding one tightly knit cell.[^cite_note-elZalaf_24-25-90] Only the most committed members,[^cite_note-Frampton_69-84] mostly young students or men with salaried jobs,[^cite_note-elZalaf_24-25-90] were invited to join. New members of the "Secret Apparatus" were taught to obey, were given weapons,[^cite_note-Frampton_69-84] underwent heavy physical training and were taught the concepts of Jihad and underground operations. The result was a zealous elite force.[^cite_note-elZalaf_24-25-90] Its first operation was allegedly towards the end of World War II, when members of the group threw a bomb at a British club.[^cite_note-Frampton_69-84] Militarized youth sections were also raised, namely the junior *kashafa* ("scouts") and the more senior *jawala* ("travellers").[^cite_note-91]

&#x20;In 1948, al-Banna denounced fascism and militarism in his book *Peace in Islam*:

> "Nazism came to power in Germany, Fascism in Italy and both Hitler and Mussolini began to force their people to conform to what they thought; unity, order, development, and power. Certainly, this system led the two countries to stability and a vital international role. This cultivated much hope, reawakened aspiration, and united the whole country under one leader. Then what happened? It became apparent that these seemingly powerful systems were a real disaster. The inspiration and aspirations of the people were shattered and the system of democracy did not lead to the empowerment of the people but to the establishment of chosen tyrants. Eventually after a deadly war in which innumerable men, women, and children died, these regimes collapsed."[^cite_note-92]

### Post–World War II

#### Conflict in Palestine and Egypt (1945–1952)

After the war the Brotherhood lobbied[^cite_note-93] for granting Amin al-Husseini, who had served as a German propaganda mouthpiece between 1941 and 1945,[^cite_note-94] asylum in Egypt. In May 1946 al-Husseini managed to escape from French imprisonment and arrived in Cairo. He received a warm welcome, especially by al-Banna, who ennobled him to a "miracle of a man" with "a divine spark in his heart which makes him above human beings", followed by a martial pledge of loyalty against the Zionists.[^cite_note-95] Soon after his arrival the [Arab League](52625.md) established the Arab Higher Executive (rebranded as [Arab Higher Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Higher_Committee) in January 1947) as supreme Palestinian party with Amin al-Husseini as Cairo-based chairman.[^cite_note-96] al-Banna designated al-Husseini a local Brotherhood leader to spread the influence of the new Palestinian branch established in October 1945.[^cite_note-97] Another small branch was founded in Jordan at the turn of 1946.[^cite_note-98]



Muslim Brotherhood fighters in the [1948 Arab–Israeli War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_War)

The Brotherhood opposed the [UN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN)'s involvement in Palestine from April 1947, with the latter eventually voting for its partition into a Jewish and an Arab state in November 1947. Consequently, the society prepared for war,[^cite_note-El-Awaisi_193-196-99] with volunteers entering Palestine as early as October 1947.[^cite_note-100] By 30 November 1947 Palestine descended into a [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947%E2%80%931948_civil_war_in_Mandatory_Palestine) fought between the [Yishuv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yishuv) and al-Husseini's Arab Higher Committee, eventually ending in a Palestinian defeat by May 1948.[^cite_note-101] Of the 10,000 fighters al-Banna had promised in October 1947[^cite_note-El-Awaisi_193-196-99] some 1.500 were present by March 1948.[^cite_note-102] The fighters came from all three branches and were originally engaged in guerrilla activities.[^cite_note-El-Awaisi_208-209-103] The end of [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine) and Israel's [declaration of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Declaration_of_Independence) on 14 May resulted in an [invasion by five Arab states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_War) in 15 May, among them Egypt.[^cite_note-104] Brotherhood fighters assisted the Egyptian army northeast of [Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_strip), although some were also active in the West Bank.[^cite_note-El-Awaisi_208-209-103] The volunteers suffered a few hundred casualties dead and wounded and had only a limited impact on the course of the war,[^cite_note-Frampton_136-105] although they played a decisive role in several engagements.[^cite_note-El-Awaisi_208-210-106] The war was an Arab failure, resulting in a truce fiercely opposed by the Muslim Brothers.[^cite_note-Frampton_136-105][^cite_note-El-Awaisi_208-210-106]



Prime minister [Nokrashy Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_El_Nokrashy_Pasha), who was shot by a Brotherhood assassin after outlawing the society in 1948

On 2 November 1945 the Brotherhood organized a general strike protesting the Balfour declaration that eventually escalated into [deadly riots targeting Jews and foreigners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1945_anti-Jewish_riots_in_Egypt).[^cite_note-107]

In March 1948 the "Secret Apparatus" assassinated a respected judge for issuing a life sentence against a Muslim Brother for attacking British soldiers.[^cite_note-108] In late 1948 the Brotherhood was estimated to have 2,000 branches and 500,000 members or sympathizers.[^cite_note-109] In November, following several bombings and alleged assassination attempts by the Brotherhood, the Egyptian government arrested 32 leaders of the Brotherhood's "Secret Apparatus" and banned the Brotherhood.[^cite_note-110] It was accused of preparing the overthrow of the government, linked to a jeep loaded with weapons.[^cite_note-Frampton_139-140-111] The headquarters were closed and its funds confiscated, while 4,000 Brothers were detained[^cite_note-elZalaf_38-39-112] and al-Banna was placed under temporary house arrest.[^cite_note-Frampton_139-140-111] The reaction to the dissolution was the assassination of prime minister [Mahmoud El Nokrashy Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_El_Nokrashy_Pasha) on 28 December by a young "Secret Apparatus" member.[^cite_note-elZalaf_38-39-112] al-Banna claimed that the killer acted independently[^cite_note-113] and publicly denounced his faith.[^cite_note-114] After a failed yet lethal bombing in mid-January 1949 which was intended to destroy legal evidence pending against the Brotherhood[^cite_note-115] al-Banna himself was killed in 12 February[^cite_note-elZalaf_38-39-112] by vengeful Nokrashy supporters.[^cite_note-116]

In 1952, members of the Muslim Brotherhood were accused of taking part in the [Cairo Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo_Fire) that destroyed some 750 buildings in downtown Cairo – mainly night clubs, theatres, hotels, and restaurants frequented by British and other foreigners.[^cite_note-RebellionWright-117]

#### Dissolution under the "Free Officers" and Nasser (1952–1970)



Brotherhood theorist [Sayyid Qutb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayyid_Qutb), who was executed in 1966

In 1952 Egypt's monarchy was [overthrown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_revolution_of_1952) by a group of nationalist military officers ([Free Officers Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Officers_Movement_\(Egypt\))) who had formed a cell within the Brotherhood during the first war against Israel in 1948. However, after the revolution [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser), the leader of the 'free officers' cell, after deposing the first president of Egypt, [Muhammad Neguib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Neguib), in a coup, quickly moved against the Brotherhood, blaming them for an attempt on his life. The Brotherhood was again banned and this time thousands of its members were imprisoned, many being tortured and held for years in prisons and [concentration camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concentration_camp). In the 1950s and 1960s many Brotherhood members sought sanctuary in Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-118] From the 1950s, al-Banna's son-in-law [Said Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Said_Ramadan) emerged as a major leader of the Brotherhood and the movement's unofficial foreign minister. Ramadan built a major center for the Brotherhood centered on a mosque in Munich, which became "a refuge for the beleaguered group during its decades in the wilderness".[^cite_note-119] In the 1960s, Ramadan worked closely with [Mahmoud K. Muftić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_K._Mufti%C4%87), a Bosnian Muslim exile who had married his cousin, who helped build links between the Muslim Brotherhood and various anti-communist exile groups in Europe and the Middle East.[^cite_note-Johnson-120][^cite_note-Mišur-121]

In the 1970s after the death of Nasser and under the new President ([Anwar Sadat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anwar_Sadat)), the Egyptian Brotherhood was invited back to Egypt and began a new phase of participation in Egyptian politics.[^cite_note-122]

### Mubarak era

During the Mubarak era, observers both defended and criticized the Brotherhood. It was the largest opposition group in Egypt, calling for "Islamic reform", and a democratic system in Egypt. It had built a vast network of support through Islamic charities working among poor Egyptians.[^cite_note-123] According to ex-[Knesset](74656.md) member and author [Uri Avnery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uri_Avnery) the Brotherhood was religious but pragmatic, "deeply embedded in Egyptian history, more Arab and more Egyptian than fundamentalist". It formed "an old established party which has earned much respect with its steadfastness in the face of recurrent persecution, torture, mass arrests and occasional executions. Its leaders are untainted by the prevalent corruption, and admired for their commitment to social work".[^cite_note-124] It also developed a significant movement online.[^cite_note-arabmediasociety.com-125][^cite_note-126]

In the [2005 parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Egyptian_parliamentary_election), the Brotherhood became "in effect, the first opposition party of Egypt's modern era". Despite electoral irregularities, including the arrest of hundreds of Brotherhood members, and having to run its candidates as independents (the organization being technically illegal), the Brotherhood won 88 seats (20% of the total) compared to 14 seats for the legal opposition.[^cite_note-nytimes2007-127]

During its term in parliament, the Brotherhood "posed a democratic political challenge to the regime, not a theological one", according to one *[The New York Times](30680.md)* journalist,[^cite_note-nytimes2007-127] while another report praised it for attempting to transform "the Egyptian parliament into a real legislative body", that represented citizens and kept the government "accountable".[^cite_note-nytimes2007-127][^cite_note-merip2006-128]

But fears remained about its commitment to democracy, equal rights, and freedom of expression and belief—or lack thereof.[^cite_note-129] In December 2006, a campus demonstration by Brotherhood students in uniforms, demonstrating martial arts drills, betrayed to some such as Jameel Theyabi, "the group's intent to plan for the creation of militia structures, and a return by the group to the era of 'secret cells'".[^cite_note-130] Another report highlighted the Muslim Brotherhood's efforts in Parliament to combat what one member called the "current US-led war against Islamic culture and identity," forcing the Minister of Culture at the time, [Farouk Hosny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farouk_Hosny), to ban the publication of three novels on the ground they promoted blasphemy and unacceptable sexual practices.[^cite_note-131] In October 2007, the Muslim Brotherhood issued a detailed political platform. Among other things, it called for a board of Muslim clerics to oversee the government, and limiting the office of the presidency to Muslim men. In the "Issues and Problems" chapter of the platform, it declared that a woman was not suited to be president because the office's religious and military duties "conflict with her nature, social and other humanitarian roles". While proclaiming "equality between men and women in terms of their human dignity", the document warned against "burdening women with duties against their nature or role in the family".[^cite_note-132]

Internally, some leaders in the Brotherhood disagreed on whether to adhere to Egypt's 32-year peace treaty with Israel. A deputy leader declared the Brotherhood would seek dissolution of the treaty,[^cite_note-133] while a Brotherhood spokesman stated the Brotherhood would respect the treaty as long as "Israel shows real progress on improving the lot of the Palestinians".[^cite_note-134]

### 2011 revolution and after

Further information: [2011 Egyptian revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Egyptian_revolution), [Freedom and Justice Party (Egypt)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_and_Justice_Party_\(Egypt\)), and [2013 Egyptian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Egyptian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat)

Following the [2011 Egyptian revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Egyptian_revolution) and fall of [Hosni Mubarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosni_Mubarak), the Brotherhood was legalized[^cite_note-MBSharia-135] and was at first very successful, dominating the [2011 parliamentary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011%E2%80%9312_Egyptian_parliamentary_election) and winning the [2012 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Egyptian_presidential_election), before the [overthrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Egyptian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) of President [Mohamed Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Morsi) a year later, leading to a crackdown on the Brotherhood again.

On 30 April 2011, the Brotherhood launched a new party called the [Freedom and Justice Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_and_Justice_Party_\(Egypt\)), which won 235 of the 498 seats in the [2011 Egyptian parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011%E2%80%9312_Egyptian_parliamentary_election), far more than any other party.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-Souaiaia,_Ahmed-137] The party rejected the "candidacy of women or Copts for Egypt's presidency", but not for cabinet positions.[^cite_note-138]



Then-U.S. secretary of state [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry) meeting with then-Egyptian president [Mohamed Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Morsi), May 2013

The Muslim Brotherhood's candidate for Egypt's 2012 presidential election was [Mohamed Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Morsi), who defeated [Ahmed Shafiq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Shafiq)—the last prime minister under Mubarak's rule—with 51.73% of the vote.[^cite_note-139] Although during his campaign Morsi himself promised to stand for peaceful relations with Israel,[^cite_note-CNN-140] some high level supporters and former Brotherhood officials (from the organization's 15-member Guidance Council) reiterated hostility towards Zionism.[^cite_note-ADL-141] For example, Egyptian cleric [Safwat Hegazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safwat_Hegazi) spoke at the announcement rally for the Muslim Brotherhood's candidate Morsi and expressed his hope and belief that Morsi would liberate Gaza, restore the Caliphate of the "United States of the Arabs" with [Jerusalem](16043.md) as its capital, and that "our cry shall be: 'Millions of martyrs march towards Jerusalem.'" Within a short period, serious public opposition developed to President Morsi. In late November 2012, he temporarily granted himself the power to [legislate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislate) without judicial oversight or review of his acts, on the grounds that he needed to protect the nation from the Mubarak-era power structure.[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143] He also put a draft constitution to a [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Egyptian_constitutional_referendum) that opponents complained was "an Islamist coup".[^cite_note-rule-144] These issues[^cite_note-alarab912-145]—and concerns over the prosecutions of journalists, the unleashing of pro-Brotherhood gangs on nonviolent demonstrators, the continuation of military trials, new laws that permitted detention without judicial review for up to 30 days,[^cite_note-continue-146] brought hundreds of thousands of [protesters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012%E2%80%932013_Egyptian_protests) to the streets starting in November 2012.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148]

By April 2013, Egypt had "become increasingly divided" between President Mohamed Morsi and "Islamist allies" and an opposition of "moderate Muslims, Christians and liberals". Opponents accused "Morsi and the Muslim Brotherhood of seeking to monopolize power, while Morsi's allies say the opposition is trying to destabilize the country to derail the elected leadership".[^cite_note-fray-149] Adding to the unrest were severe fuel shortages and electricity outages, which raised suspicions among some Egyptians that the end of gas and electricity shortages since the ouster of President Mohamed Morsi was evidence of a conspiracy to undermine him, although other Egyptians say it was evidence of Morsi's mismanagement of the economy.[^cite_note-150]

On 3 July 2013, Mohamed Morsi was removed from office and put into house arrest by the military,[^cite_note-151] that happened shortly after [mass protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_2013_Egyptian_protests) against him began.[^cite_note-misunderstood-quote-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156] demanding the resignation of Morsi. There were also significant counter-protests in support of Morsi;[^cite_note-huffingtonpost.com-157] those were originally intended to celebrate the one-year anniversary of Morsi's inauguration, and started days before the uprising. On 14 August, the interim government declared a month-long state of emergency, and riot police cleared the pro-Morsi sit-in during the [Rabaa sit-in dispersal of August 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_2013_Rabaa_massacre). Violence escalated rapidly following armed protesters attacking police, according to the [National Council for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Council_for_Human_Rights)' report;[^cite_note-158] this led to the deaths of over 600 people and injury of some 4,000,[^cite_note-ahram1-159][^cite_note-160] with the incident resulting in the most casualties in Egypt's modern history.[^cite_note-161] In retaliation, Brotherhood supporters looted and burned police stations and dozens of churches in response to the violence, though a Muslim Brotherhood spokesperson condemned the attacks on Christians and instead blamed military leaders for plotting the attacks.[^cite_note-churches-162] The crackdown that followed has been called the worst for the Brotherhood's organization "in eight decades".[^cite_note-run-163] By 19 August, [Al Jazeera](30204052.md) reported that "most" of the Brotherhood's leaders were in custody.[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165] On that day Supreme Leader [Mohammed Badie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Badie) was arrested,[^cite_note-166] crossing a "red line", as even [Hosni Mubarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosni_Mubarak) had never arrested him.[^cite_note-badie-167] On 23 September, a court ordered the group outlawed and its assets seized.[^cite_note-shut-168] [Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Egypt), [Hazem Al Beblawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazem_Al_Beblawi) on 21 December 2013, declared the Muslim Brotherhood as a terrorist organisation after a car bomb ripped through a police building and killed at least 14 people in the city of [Mansoura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansoura,_Egypt), which the government blamed on the Muslim Brotherhood, despite no evidence and an unaffiliated [Sinai-based terror group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ansar_Beit_al-Maqdis) claiming responsibility for the attack.[^cite_note-169]



A group of pro-Brotherhood protesters holding the [Rabia sign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabia_sign) and making the associated gesture during a pro-Brotherhood protest held in October 2013

On 24 March 2014, an Egyptian court sentenced 529 members of the Muslim Brotherhood [to death](5902.md)[^cite_note-170] following an attack on a police station, an act described by [Amnesty International](18947898.md) as "the largest single batch of simultaneous death sentences we've seen in recent years \[...] anywhere in the world".[^cite_note-171] By May 2014, approximately 16,000 people (and as high as more than 40,000 by what *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* calls an "independent count"),[^cite_note-172] mostly Brotherhood members or supporters, have allegedly been arrested by police since the 2013 uprising.[^cite_note-173] On 2 February 2015, an Egyptian court sentenced another 183 members of the Muslim Brotherhood to death.[^cite_note-174]

An editorial in *[The New York Times](30680.md)* claimed that "leaders of the Muslim Brotherhood, which became the leading political movement in the wake of Egypt's 2011 popular uprising, are languishing in prison, unfairly branded as terrorists. ... Egypt's crushing authoritarianism could well persuade a significant number of its citizens that violence is the only tool they have for fighting back".[^cite_note-175]

Mohamed Morsi was sentenced to death on 16 May 2015, along with 120 others.[^cite_note-176]

The Muslim Brotherhood claimed that Muslims did not carry out the [Botroseya Church bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botroseya_Church_bombing) and claimed it was a false flag conspiracy by the Egyptian government and Copts, in a statement released in Arabic on the FJP's website,[^cite_note-177] but its claim was challenged by [100 Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/100_Women_\(BBC\)) participant Nervana Mahmoud[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] and Hoover Institution and Hudson Institute fellow Samuel Tadros.[^cite_note-180] The Muslim Brotherhood released an English-language commentary on the bombing and said it condemned the terrorist attack.[^cite_note-181]

Qatar-based Muslim Brotherhood members are suspected to have helped a Muslim Brotherhood agent carry out the bombing, according to the Egyptian government.[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184] The Qatar-based supporter was named as Mohab Mostafa El-Sayed Qassem.[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187] The terrorist was named as Mahmoud Shafiq Mohamed Mostaf.[^cite_note-188]

The Arabic-language website of the Muslim Brotherhood commemorated the anniversary of the death of its leader, Hassan al-Banna, and repeated his words calling for the teachings of Islam to spread all over the world and to raise the "flag of Jihad", taking their land, "regaining their glory", "including diaspora Muslims" and demanding an [Islamic state](2646438.md) and a Muslim government, a Muslim people, a Muslim house, and Muslim individuals.[^cite_note-189]

[Mekameleen TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mekameleen_TV), a Turkey-based free-to-air satellite television channel run by exiled Brotherhood supporters, mourned his death and claimed it was "martyrdom". Mekameleen supports the Brotherhood.[^cite_note-190] Condolences were sent upon Omar Abdel Rahman's death by the website of the Muslim Brotherhood's Freedom and Justice Party in Egypt.[^cite_note-191]

### Controversy

How much of the blame for the fall from power in Egypt of the Brotherhood and its allied Freedom and Justice Party (FJP) can be placed on the Brotherhood, and how much of it can be placed on its enemies in the Egyptian bureaucracy, media and security establishment is disputed. The Mubarak government's state media portrayed the Brotherhood as secretive and illegal,[^cite_note-192] and numerous TV channels such as OnTV spent much of their air time vilifying the organization.[^cite_note-193] But the Brotherhood took a number of controversial steps and also acquiesced to or supported crackdowns by the military during Morsi's presidency.[^cite_note-194] Before the revolution, the Muslim Brotherhood's supporters appeared at a protest at Al-Azhar University wearing military-style fatigues, after which the Mubarak government accused the organization of starting an underground militia.[^cite_note-195] When it came to power, the Muslim Brotherhood indeed tried to establish armed groups of supporters and it sought official permission for its members to be armed.[^cite_note-196]
