[Mohammed Badie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Badie), the current leader

- Founder and first General Leader: (1928–1949) [Hassan al-Banna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_al-Banna) حسن البنا

- 1949–1972 [Hassan al-Hudaybi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_al-Hudaybi) حسن الهضيبي

- 1972–1986 [Umar al-Tilmisani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar_al-Tilmisani) عمر التلمساني

- 1986–1996 [Muhammad Hamid Abu al-Nasr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Hamid_Abu_al-Nasr) محمد حامد أبو النصر

- 1996–2002 [Mustafa Mashhur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Mashhur) مصطفى مشهور

- 2002–2004 [Ma'mun al-Hudaybi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma'mun_al-Hudaybi) مأمون الهضيبي

- 2004–2010 [Mohammed Mahdi Akef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Mahdi_Akef) محمد مهدي عاكف

- 2010– [Mohammed Badie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Badie) محمد بديع

## Presence outside of Egypt

### In the Middle East

#### Bahrain

Further information: [Al Eslah Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Eslah_Society) and [Al-Menber Islamic Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Menber_Islamic_Society)

Following parliamentary elections in 2002, [Al Menbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Menbar_Islamic_Society) became the largest joint party with eight seats in the forty-seat [Chamber of Deputies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Deputies_of_Bahrain). Prominent members of Al Menbar include Dr. Salah Abdulrahman, Dr. Salah Al Jowder, and outspoken MP [Mohammed Khalid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Khalid). Additionally, it has strongly opposed the government's accession to the [International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Covenant_on_Civil_and_Political_Rights).[^cite_note-197]

#### Iran

See also: [Iranian Call and Reform Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Call_and_Reform_Organization)

[Iranian Call and Reform Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Call_and_Reform_Organization), a Sunni Islamist group active in Iran, predominantly among Kurds, has been described as an organization "that belongs to the Muslim Brotherhood"[^cite_note-198] or "Iranian Muslim Brotherhood",[^cite_note-199] while it has officially stated that it is not affiliated with the latter.[^cite_note-200]

#### Turkey

Further information: [Muslim Brotherhood in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_Brotherhood_in_Turkey)



Erdoğan performing the [Rabaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabia_sign) gesture (which is used by Muslim Brotherhood supporters in Egypt protesting against the post-Brotherhood authorities)

The Turkish [AKP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Development_Party_\(Turkey\)), the ruling party of Turkey, publicly supported the Muslim Brotherhood during and a few months after the [overthrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Egyptian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) of the Muslim Brotherhood-affiliated Egyptian president [Mohamed Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Morsi) in July 2013.[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202] Then-Turkish prime minister [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan) claimed in an interview that this was because "Turkey would stand by whoever was elected as a result of legitimate elections."[^cite_note-203] According to the [Carnegie Endowment for International Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Endowment_for_International_Peace), each year after Morsi's overthrow has seen the AKP "significantly detach itself from the Muslim Brotherhood in Egypt."[^cite_note-204]

#### Iraq

Further information: [Muslim Brotherhood in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_Brotherhood_in_Iraq), [Hamas of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamas_of_Iraq), [Kurdistan Islamic Union](2962116.md), and [Iraqi Islamic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Islamic_Party)

The [Iraqi Islamic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Islamic_Party) was formed in 1960 as the Iraqi branch of the Brotherhood,[^cite_note-205] but was banned from 1961 during the nationalist rule of [Abd al-Karim Qasim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Karim_Qasim). As government repression hardened under the [Baath Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baath_Party) from February 1963, the group was forced to continue underground. After the [fall](201936.md) of the [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) government in 2003, the Islamic Party has reemerged as one of the main advocates of the country's Sunni community. Its leader is Iraqi Vice-president [Tariq Al-Hashimi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tariq_Al-Hashimi).

The Muslim Brotherhood was an active participation in the "Faith Campaign".[^cite_note-206]

[Khaled al-Obaidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaled_al-Obaidi) said that he received a death threat and was declared a non-Muslim by the Muslim Brotherhood.[^cite_note-207]

Also, in the north of Iraq there are several Islamic movements inspired by or part of the Muslim Brotherhood network. The [Kurdistan Islamic Union](2962116.md) (KIU), a small political party holding 10 seats in the Kurdish parliament, was believed to be supportive of the Muslim Brotherhood in the 90's.[^cite_note-208] The group leaders and members have been continuously arrested by Kurdish authorities.

#### Israel

Further information: [Islamic Movement in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Movement_in_Israel)

'Abd al-Rahman al-Banna, the brother of the Muslim Brotherhood founder Hasan al-Banna, went to [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine) and established the Muslim Brotherhood there in 1935. [Al-Hajj Amin al-Husseini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Amin_al-Husayni), eventually appointed by the British as Grand Mufti of Jerusalem in hopes of accommodating him, was the leader of the group in Palestine.[^cite_note-209] Another important leader associated with the Muslim Brotherhood in Palestine was 'Izz al-Din al-Qassam, an inspiration to Islamists because he had been the first to lead an armed resistance in the name of Palestine against the British in 1935.[^cite_note-210]

Brotherhood members fought alongside the Arab armies during the [1948 Arab–Israeli war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_war), and, after Israel's creation, the ensuing [Palestinian refugee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_refugee) crisis encouraged more Palestinian Muslims to join the group. After the war, in the West Bank, the group's activity was mainly social and religious, not political, so it had relatively good relations with Jordan during the [Jordanian annexation of the West Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordanian_annexation_of_the_West_Bank). In contrast, the group frequently clashed with the Egyptian government that controlled the Gaza Strip until 1967.[^cite_note-211]

In the 1950s and 1960s, the Brotherhood's goal was "the upbringing of an Islamic generation" through the restructuring of society and religious education, rather than opposition to Israel, and so it lost popularity to insurgent movements and the presence of [Hizb ut-Tahrir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hizb_ut-Tahrir).[^cite_note-212] Eventually, however, the Brotherhood was strengthened by several factors:

1. The creation of al-Mujamma' al-Islami, the Islamic Center in 1973 by Shaykh Ahmad Yasin had a centralizing effect that encapsulated all religious organizations.

2. The Muslim Brotherhood Society in Jordan and Palestine was created from a merger of the branches in the West Bank and Gaza and Jordan.

3. Palestinian disillusion with the Palestinian militant groups caused them to become more open to alternatives.

4. The Islamic Revolution in Iran offered inspiration to Palestinians. The Brotherhood was able to increase its efforts in Palestine and avoid being dismantled like militant groups because it did not focus on the occupation. While militant groups were being dismantled, the Brotherhood filled the void.

   [^cite_note-hamas-213]

In 2006, the Brotherhood supported [Hezbollah's military action against Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Lebanon_War). It does not recognize the State of Israel.[^cite_note-214]

#### Palestine

Further information: [Hamas](13913.md)

Between 1967 and 1987, the year Hamas was founded, the number of mosques in Gaza tripled from 200 to 600, and the Muslim Brotherhood named the period between 1975 and 1987 a phase of "social institution building."[^cite_note-Strindberg,_Anders,_and_Wärn,_Mats_2011_87-11] During that time, the Brotherhood established associations, used [zakat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakat) (alms giving) for aid to poor Palestinians, promoted schools, provided students with loans, used [waqf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waqf) (religious endowments) to lease property and employ people, and established mosques. Likewise, antagonistic and sometimes violent opposition to [Fatah](11104.md), the [Palestine Liberation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Liberation_Organization) and other secular nationalist groups increased dramatically in the streets and on university campuses.[^cite_note-online.wsj.com-215]

In 1987, following the First Intifada, the *Islamic Resistance Movement*, or [Hamas](13913.md)[^cite_note-hamas-213][^cite_note-216] was established from Brotherhood-affiliated charities and social institutions that had gained a strong foothold among the local population. During the [First Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intifada) (1987–93), Hamas militarized and transformed into one of the strongest Palestinian militant groups.

The [Hamas takeover of the Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Gaza_\(2007\)) in 2007 was the first time since the Sudanese coup of 1989 that brought [Omar al-Bashir](393625.md) to power, that a Muslim Brotherhood group ruled a significant geographic territory.[^cite_note-Schanzer-217] However, the 2013 overthrow of the [Mohammad Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Morsi) government in Egypt significantly weakened Hamas's position, leading to a blockade of Gaza and economic crisis.[^cite_note-Ibish3-hamas-218]

#### Jordan

Further information: [Islamic Action Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Action_Front)

The Muslim Brotherhood in Jordan originates from the merging of two separate groups which represent the two components of the Jordanian public: the Transjordanian and the West Bank Palestinian.[^cite_note-The_Muslim_Brotherhood_in_Jordan-219]

On 9 November 1945 the Association of the Muslim Brotherhood (Jam'iyat al-Ikhwan al-Muslimin) was officially registered and Abu Qura became its first General Supervisor.[^cite_note-The_Muslim_Brotherhood_in_Jordan-219] Abu Qura originally brought the Brotherhood to Jordan from Egypt after extensive study and spread of the teachings of Imam Hasan al-Banna.[^cite_note-The_Muslim_Brotherhood_in_Jordan-219] While most political parties and movements were banned for a long time in Jordan such as [Hizb ut-Tahrir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hizb_ut-Tahrir), the Brotherhood was exempted and allowed to operate by the Jordanian monarchy. In 1948, Egypt, Syria, and Transjordan offered "volunteers" to help Palestine in its war against Israel. Due to the defeat and weakening of Palestine, the Transjordanian and Palestinian Brotherhood merged.[^cite_note-The_Muslim_Brotherhood_in_Jordan-219]

The newly merged Muslim Brotherhood in Jordan was primarily concerned with providing social services and charitable work as well as with politics and its role in the parliament. It was seen as compatible with the political system and supported democracy without the forced implementation of Sharia law which was part of its doctrine.[^cite_note-220] However, internal pressures from younger members of the Brotherhood who called for more militant actions as well as his failing health, Abu Qura resigned as the leader of the Jordanian Muslim Brotherhood. On 26 December 1953, Muhammad 'Abd al-Rahman Khalifa, was elected by the movement's administrative committee as the new leader of the Transjordanian Brotherhood and he retained this position until 1994. Khalifa was different from his predecessor and older members of the organization because he was not educated in Cairo, he was educated in Syria and Palestine. He established close ties with Palestinian Islamists during his educational life which led him to be jailed for several months in Jordan for criticizing Arab armies in the war.[^cite_note-The_Muslim_Brotherhood_in_Jordan-219]

Khalifa also reorganized the Brotherhood and applied to the government to designate the Brotherhood as "a comprehensive and general Islamic Committee, instead of the previous basis of operation under the Societies and Clubs Law". This allowed the Brotherhood to spread throughout the country each with slight socioeconomic and political differences although the majority of the members were of the upper middle class. The radicalization of the Brotherhood began to take place after the peace process between Egypt and Israel, the Islamic Revolution of Iran, as well as their open criticism towards the Jordan-US relationship in the 1970s. Support for the Syrian branch of the Brotherhood also aided the radicalization of the group through open support and training for the rebel forces in Syria. The ideology began to transform into a more militant one which without it would not have the support of the Islamic radicals.[^cite_note-221]

The Jordanian Brotherhood has formed its own political party, the [Islamic Action Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Action_Front). In 1989 they become the largest group in parliament, with 23 out of 80 seats, and 9 other Islamist allies.[^cite_note-222] A Brother was elected president of the National Assembly and the cabinet formed in January 1991 included several MBs.[^cite_note-223]

In 2011, against the backdrop of the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring), the Jordanian Muslim Brotherhood "mobilized popular protests on a larger, more regular, and more oppositional basis than ever before".[^cite_note-Amis-224] and had uniquely positioned themselves as "the only traditional political actor to have remained prominent during \[the] new phase of post-Arab Spring activism"[^cite_note-Amis-224] which led [King Abdullah II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_II_of_Jordan) and then-Prime Minister [Marouf al-Bakhit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marouf_al-Bakhit) to invite the Muslim Brotherhood to join Bakhit's cabinet, an offer they refused.[^cite_note-225] The Muslim Brotherhood also boycotted the 2011 Jordanian municipal elections and led the [2011–12 Jordanian protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011%E2%80%9312_Jordanian_protests) demanding a constitutional monarchy and electoral reforms, which resulted in the firing of [Prime Minister Bakhit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marouf_al-Bakhit) and the calling of [early general elections in 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Jordanian_general_election).[^cite_note-Amis-224]

As of late 2013, the movement in Jordan was described as being in "disarray".[^cite_note-Ibish2-226] The instability and conflict with the monarchy has led the relationship between the two to crumble. In 2015, some 400 members of the Muslim Brotherhood defected from the original group including top leaders and founding members, to establish another Islamic group, with an allegedly moderate stance. The defectors said that they didn't like how things were run in the group and due to the group's relations with Hamas, Qatar and Turkey, which put suspicion on the group questioning if they are under the influence and working for the benefit of these states and organizations on the expense of the Jordanian state.[^cite_note-227]

On 13 April 2016, Jordanian police raided and shut the Muslim Brotherhood headquarters in Amman. This comes despite the fact that the Jordanian branch cut ties with the mother Egyptian group in January 2016, a designated terrorist organization, a move that is considered to be exclusively cosmetic by experts. Jordanian authorities state that the reason of closure is because that the Brotherhood is unlicensed and is using the name of the defectors' licensed group. This comes after the Jordanian senate passed a new legislation for the regulation of political parties in 2014, the Muslim Brotherhood did not adhere by the regulations of the new law and so they did not renew their membership.[^cite_note-228]

In 2020, a Jordanian Court of Cassation decided that the local branch of the Muslim Brotherhood will be dissolved after the branch did not renew its license after a new law was issued on organizations.[^cite_note-229]

On 23 April 2025, Jordan's Interior Minister [Mazin Al Farrayeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazin_Al-Farrayeh) announced the immediate ban of the Muslim Brotherhood and the seizure of its assets and offices, following the uncovering of a sabotage plot linked to members of the group. The Muslim Brotherhood has had a longstanding presence in Jordan, officially registered in 1945, and has been involved in both social services and political activities, including forming its own political party, the Islamic Action Front. Over the years, the group has experienced internal divisions and increasing tensions with the Jordanian government, leading to previous actions such as the 2016 closure of its headquarters and a 2020 court ruling ordering its dissolution for failing to renew its license. This latest move reflects the government's ongoing efforts to curb the organization's influence within the country.[^cite_note-230]

#### Qatar

In 1999 the Muslim Brotherhood was disbanded in Qatar. The country's longstanding support for the group has been often explained as determined by a strategic calculus that limited the role played by religion in Qatar.[^cite_note-\:20-231] As the director of the Center for International and Regional Studies at the Doha-based branch of Georgetown University, Mehran Kamrava, posited, Qatar presenting itself as the state patron of the Muslim Brotherhood has caused religion in Qatar to not "play any role in articulating or forming oppositional sentiments."[^cite_note-\:20-231]

Qatar's patronage has been primarily expressed through the ruling family's endorsement of Muslim Brotherhood's most representative figures, especially [Yusuf al-Qaradawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_al-Qaradawi). Qaradawi is a prominent, yet controversial Sunni preacher and theologian who continues to serve as the spiritual leader of the Muslim Brotherhood. An Egyptian citizen, Qaradawi fled Egypt for Qatar in 1961 after being imprisoned under President [Gamal Abdul Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdul_Nasser). In 1962 he chaired the Qatari Secondary Institute of Religious Studies, and in 1977 he founded and directed the Shariah and Islamic Studies department at the [University of Qatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Qatar).[^cite_note-232] He left Qatar to return to Egypt shortly before the [2011 Egyptian revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Egyptian_revolution).

For twenty years, Qaradawi has hosted a popular show titled Shariah and Life on the Qatari-based media channel [Al-Jazeera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Jazeera_Arabic), a government sponsored channel notoriously supportive of the Muslim Brotherhood and Islamism and often designated as a propaganda outlet for the Qatari government.[^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234][^cite_note-235] From that platform, he has promoted his Islamist—and often radical views—on life, politics, and culture.

His positions, as well as his controversial ties to extremist and terrorist individuals and organizations, made him [persona non grata](521241.md) to the U.S., UK and French governments respectively in 1999, 2008, and 2012.[^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237]

Before 2013, however, Qatar had made a substantial investment on Morsi's leadership and had devolved about \$10 million to Egypt since Morsi was elected, allegedly also to "buy political advantage" in the country.[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239]

In December 2019, Qatari Foreign Minister Mohammed bin Abdulrahman Al Thani told CNN in an interview that Qatar never supported Muslim Brotherhood and does not fund terrorism.[^cite_note-240]

#### Kuwait

Further information: [Hadas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadas)

Egyptian Brethren came to Kuwait in the 1950s as refugees from Arab nationalism and integrated into the education ministry and other parts of the state. The Brotherhood's charity arm in Kuwait is called Al Eslah (Social Reform Society)[^cite_note-monitor-Kuwait-241] and its political arm is called the Islamic Constitutional Movement (ICM) or "Hadas".[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243] Members of ICM have been elected to parliament and served in the government and are "widely believed to hold sway with the [Ministry of Awqaf" (Islamic endowment) and Islamic Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuwait_Ministry_of_Awqaf_and_Islamic_Affairs), but have never reached a majority or even a plurality—"a fact that has required them to be pragmatic about working with other political groups".[^cite_note-monitor-Kuwait-241] During the [invasion of Kuwait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Kuwait), the Kuwait MB (along with other MB in the Gulf States) supported the American-Saudi coalition forces against Iraq and "quit the brotherhood's international agency in protest" over its pro-Saddam stand.[^cite_note-roy-121-244] However following the Arab Spring and the crackdown on the Egyptian Brotherhood, the Saudi government has put "pressure on other states that have Muslim Brotherhood adherents, asking them to decree that the group is a terrorist organization", and the local Kuwaiti and other Gulf state Brotherhoods have not been spared pressure from their local governments.[^cite_note-monitor-Kuwait-241]

#### Saudi Arabia

The Kingdom of Saudi Arabia helped the Brotherhood financially for "over half a century",[^cite_note-WashingtonPost-Diverse-245][^cite_note-Dreyfuss-246] but the two became estranged during the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War), and enemies after the election of [Mohamed Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Morsi). Inside the kingdom, before the crushing of the Egyptian MB, the Brotherhood was called a group whose "many quiet supporters" made it "one of the few potential threats" to the royal family's control.[^cite_note-Nosat-econ2-1-2014-247]

The Brotherhood first had an impact inside Saudi Arabia in 1954 when thousands of Egyptian Brethren sought to escape president Gamal Abdel Nasser's clampdown, while (the largely illiterate) Saudi Arabia was looking for teachers—who were also conservative pious Arab Muslims—for its newly created public school system.[^cite_note-248] The Muslim Brotherhood's brand of Islam and Islamic politics differs from the [Salafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafism) creed called [Wahhabiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahhabi), officially held by the state of Saudi Arabia, and MB members "obeyed orders of the ruling family and *[ulama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama)* to not attempt to proselytize or otherwise get involved in religious doctrinal matters within the Kingdom. Nonetheless, the group "methodically ... took control of Saudi Arabia's intellectual life" by publishing books and participating in discussion circles and salons held by princes.[^cite_note-Kepel-war-249] Although the organization had no "formal organizational presence" in the Kingdom,[^cite_note-250] (no political groups or parties are allowed to operate openly)[^cite_note-Dreyfuss-246] MB members became "entrenched both in Saudi society and in the Saudi state, taking a leading role in key governmental ministries".[^cite_note-Lacroix-251] In particular, many established themselves in Saudi educational system. One expert on Saudi affairs (Stephane Lacroix) has stated: "The education system is so controlled by the Muslim Brotherhood, it will take 20 years to change—if at all. Islamists see education as their base" in Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-252]

Relations between the Saudi ruling family and the Brotherhood became strained with Saudi opposition to Iraq's invasion of Kuwait and the willingness of Saudi government to allow US troops to be based in the Kingdom to fight Iraq.[^cite_note-Lacroix-251] The Brotherhood supported the *[Sahwah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahwa_movement)* ("Awakening") movement that pushed for political change in the Kingdom.[^cite_note-McCants-253] In 2002, the then Saudi Interior Minister [Prince Nayef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nayef_bin_Abdulaziz_Al_Saud) denounced the Brotherhood, saying it was guilty of "betrayal of pledges and ingratitude" and was "the source of all problems in the Islamic world".[^cite_note-WashingtonPost-Diverse-245] The ruling family was also alarmed by the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) and the example set by the Muslim Brotherhood in Egypt, with president [Mohamed Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Morsi) bringing an Islamist government to power by means of popular revolution and elections.[^cite_note-Katulis-254] [Sahwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahwa_movement) figures published petitions for reform addressed to the royal government (in violation of Wahhabi [quietist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_quietism_in_Islam) doctrine). After the overthrow of the Morsi government in Egypt, all the major Sahwa figures signed petitions and statements denouncing the removal of Morsi and the Saudi government support for it.[^cite_note-Lacroix-251]

In March 2014, in a "significant departure from its past official stance" the Saudi government declared the Brotherhood a "terrorist organization", followed with a royal decree announced that, from now on,

> belonging to intellectual or religious trends or groups that are extremist or categorized as terrorist at the local, regional or international level, as well supporting them, or showing sympathy for their ideas and methods in whichever way, or expressing support for them through whichever means, or offering them financial or moral support, or inciting others to do any of this or promoting any such actions in word or writing

will be punished by a prison sentence "of no less than three years and no more than twenty years".[^cite_note-Lacroix-251]

#### Syria

Main article: [Muslim Brotherhood in Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_Brotherhood_in_Syria)

The Muslim Brotherhood in Syria was founded in the 1930s (according to lexicorient.com) or in 1945, a year before independence from France, (according to journalist [Robin Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Wright_\(author\))). In the first decade or so of independence it was part of the legal opposition, and in the [1961 parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1961_Syrian_parliamentary_election) it won ten seats (5.8% of the house). But after the 1963 coup that brought the secular [Ba'ath Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Socialist_Ba'ath_Party_%E2%80%93_Syria_Region) to power it was banned.[^cite_note-255] It played a major role in the mainly [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni)-based movement that opposed the [secularist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism), [pan-Arabist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arabism) Ba'ath Party. This conflict [developed into an armed struggle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamist_uprising_in_Syria) that continued until culminating in the [Hama uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Hama_massacre) of 1982, when the rebellion was crushed by the military.[^cite_note-256]

Membership in the Syrian Brotherhood became a [capital offense](5902.md) in Syria in 1980 (under Emergency Law 49, which was revoked in 2011), but the headquarters of the Muslim Brotherhood-linked Palestinian group, [Hamas](13913.md), was located in the Syria's capital Damascus, where it was given Syrian government support. This has been cited as an example of the lack of international centralization or even coordination of the Muslim Brotherhood.[^cite_note-257]

The Brotherhood is said to have "resurrected itself" and become the "dominant group" in the opposition by 2012 during the [Syrian Civil War](30741795.md) according to the *[Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Post)* newspaper.[^cite_note-nyt05122012-258] But by 2013 another source described it as having "virtually no influence on the conflict".[^cite_note-Ibish3-syrian-259] Syrian president [Bashar al-Assad](364813.md) welcomed the fall of the Muslim Brotherhood in Egypt and remarked that "Arab identity is back on the right track after the fall from power of Egypt's Muslim Brotherhood, which had used religion for its own political gain".[^cite_note-260]

A major turning point for the Muslim Brotherhood in Syria came in December 2024, when the [Ba'ath regime](57134800.md) fell and the president fled the country. The succeeding government and president have been directly linked to the Brotherhood.

#### United Arab Emirates

Further information: [Al Islah (United Arab Emirates)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Islah_\(United_Arab_Emirates\))

Muslim Brotherhood presence in the United Arab Emirates began with the formation of the [Al Islah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Islah_\(United_Arab_Emirates\)) group in the United Arab Emirates in 1974 with the approval of Sheikh [Rashid bin Saeed Al Maktoum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashid_bin_Saeed_Al_Maktoum).[^cite_note-reuters-261]

Al Islah in the UAE has openly stated that it shares ideology with the Muslim Brotherhood in Egypt.[^cite_note-news.yahoo-262] Al Islah has criticized the UAE for the country's [religious tolerance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_tolerance) and presence of community Christian [churches](6325.md) in the UAE.[^cite_note-263] Since its formation, its members have sought to impose control on state social issues, such as promoting several measures limiting the [rights of women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rights_of_women).[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265] Emirati Al Islah member Tharwat Kherbawi said the Muslim Brotherhood finds the present UAE government to be an "impediment", and the country itself to be a "treasure and a crucial strategic and economic prize".[^cite_note-IBISH-266]

Al Islah was reported to have been secretly forming a military wing that has sought to recruit retired [military officers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_officers) and young [Emiratis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emiratis) and is alleged to have plotted the overthrow of the current government and the establishment of an [Islamist](15012.md) state in the UAE.[^cite_note-reuters-261][^cite_note-267]

In March 2013, a trial began in [Abu Dhabi](18950756.md) for 94 individuals linked to Al Islah for an attempted coup on the government.[^cite_note-268] Of the 94, 56 suspects received prison sentences ranging between three and ten years. Eight suspects were sentenced in absentia to 15 years in jail and 26 were acquitted.[^cite_note-269]

On 7 March 2014, the Muslim Brotherhood was designated as a terrorist group by the UAE government.[^cite_note-Alaa_Shahine_and_Glen_Carey,_Bloomberg_News-54]

#### Yemen

Further information: [Al-Islah (Yemen)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Islah_\(Yemen\))

The Muslim Brothers fought with North Yemen in the NDF rebellion as Islamic Front. The Muslim Brotherhood is the political arm of the [Yemeni Congregation for Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemeni_Congregation_for_Reform), commonly known as [Al-Islah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Islah_\(Yemen\)). Former president [Ali Abdullah Saleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Abdullah_Saleh) made substantial efforts to entrench the accusations of being in league with Al Qaeda.[^cite_note-270]

The Treasury Department of the US used the label "Bin Laden loyalist" for [Abdul Majeed al-Zindani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Majeed_al-Zindani), the Yemeni Muslim Brotherhood's leader.[^cite_note-271]

### Rest of Africa

#### Algeria

Further information: [Movement of Society for Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movement_of_Society_for_Peace)

The Muslim Brotherhood reached Algeria during the later years of the French colonial presence in the country (1830–1962). Sheikh Ahmad Sahnoun led the organization in Algeria between 1953 and 1954 during the French colonialism. Brotherhood members and sympathizers took part in the uprising against France in 1954–1962, but the movement was marginalized during the largely secular FLN one-party rule which was installed at independence in 1962. It remained unofficially active, sometimes protesting the government and calling for increased Islamization and Arabization of the country's politics.[^cite_note-272]

When a multi-party system was introduced in Algeria in the early 1990s, the Muslim Brotherhood formed the [Movement of Society for Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movement_of_Society_for_Peace) (MSP, previously known as Hamas), led by [Mahfoud Nahnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahfoud_Nahnah) until his death in 2003 (he was succeeded by present party leader [Boudjerra Soltani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boudjerra_Soltani)). The Muslim Brotherhood in Algeria did not join the [Front islamique du salut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Front_islamique_du_salut) (FIS), which emerged as the leading Islamist group, winning the 1991 elections and which was banned in 1992 following a military coup d'état, although some Brotherhood sympathizers did. The Brotherhood subsequently also refused to join the violent post-coup uprising by FIS sympathizers and the [Armed Islamic Groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Islamic_Groups) (GIA) against the Algerian state and military which followed, and urged a peaceful resolution to the conflict and a return to democracy. It has thus remained a legal political organization and enjoyed parliamentary and government representation. In 1995, Sheikh Nahnah ran for [President of Algeria](380664.md) finishing second with 25.38% of the popular vote. During the 2000s (decade), the party—led by Nahnah's successor [Boudjerra Soltani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boudjerra_Soltani)—has been a member of a three-party coalition backing President [Abdelaziz Bouteflika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdelaziz_Bouteflika).

#### Libya

Further information: [Justice and Construction Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Construction_Party), [Party of Reform and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_Reform_and_Development), and [Homeland Party (Libya)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeland_Party_\(Libya\))

A group of the Muslim Brotherhood came to the Libyan kingdom in the 1950s as refugees escaping crackdown by the Egyptian leader [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser), but it was not able to operate openly until after the [First Libyan Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Libyan_Civil_War). They were viewed negatively by King [Idris of Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idris_of_Libya) who had become increasingly wary of their activities. [Muammar Gaddafi](53029.md) forbade all forms of [Islamism](15012.md) in Libya and was an archenemy to the Muslim Brotherhood for long time. The group held its first public press conference on 17 November 2011, and on 24 December the Brotherhood announced that it would form the [Justice and Construction Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Construction_Party) (JCP) and contest the [General National Congress elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Libyan_General_National_Congress_election) the following year.[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] The Libyan Muslim Brotherhood has "little history of interactions with the masses."[^cite_note-275]

Despite predictions based on fellow post-[Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) nations Tunisia and Egypt that the Brotherhood's party would easily win the elections, it instead came a distant second to the [National Forces Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Forces_Alliance), receiving just 10% of the vote and 17 out of 80 [party-list](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party-list_proportional_representation) seats.[^cite_note-276] Their candidate for Prime Minister, [Awad al-Baraasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Awad_al-Baraasi) was also defeated in the first round of voting in September, although he was later made a Deputy Prime Minister under [Ali Zeidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Zeidan).[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278] A JCP Congressman, Saleh Essaleh is also the vice speaker of the [General National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_National_Congress).[^cite_note-279]

The Party of Reform and Development is led by Khaled al-Werchefani, a former member of the Muslim Brotherhood.[^cite_note-islamistparty-280]

Sallabi, the Head of Homeland Party, has close ties to [Yusuf al-Qaradawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_al-Qaradawi), the spiritual leader of the international Muslim Brotherhood.[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282]

The Muslim Brotherhood in Libya has come under widespread criticism, particularly for their alleged ties with extremist organizations operating in Libya.[^cite_note-283] In fact, the text of the U.S. Congress Muslim Brotherhood Terrorist Designation Act of 2015 directly accuses the militias of the Libyan Muslim Brotherhood of "joining forces with United States designated terrorist organizations, particularly Ansar al-Sharia" who the United States blames for the attack on its compound in Benghazi.[^cite_note-284][^cite_note-285] There have been similar reports that those tasked with guarding the Benghazi consulate on the night of the assault were connected to the Muslim Brotherhood.[^cite_note-286]

The Libyan Muslim Brotherhood has lost much of its popular support since 2012 as the group was blamed for divisions in the country. Secular Libyan politicians have continued to voice concerns of the Brotherhood's ties to extremist groups. In October 2017, spokesman of the Libyan National Army (LNA) colonel Ahmed Al Masmary claimed that "branches of the Muslim Brotherhood affiliated to al-Qaeda" had joined forces with ISIS in Libya.[^cite_note-287] In the 2014 parliamentary elections, the Muslim Brotherhood won only 25 of the 200 available seats.[^cite_note-288]

#### Mauritania

Further information: [National Rally for Reform and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rally_for_Reform_and_Development)

Changes to the demographic and political makeup of Mauritania in the 1970s heavily contributed to the growth of Islamism within Mauritanian society. Periods of [severe drought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahel_drought#Late_20th_century_droughts) resulted in urbanization, as large numbers of Mauritanians moved from the countryside to the cities, particularly [Nouakchott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nouakchott), to escape the drought. This sharp increase in urbanization resulted in new civil associations being formed, and Mauritania's first Islamist organisation, known as Jemaa Islamiyya (Islamic Association) was formed by Mauritanians sympathetic to the Muslim Brotherhood.[^cite_note-Mauritania_Carnegie-289]

There was increased activism relating to the Muslim Brotherhood in the 1980s, partially driven by members of the Egyptian Muslim Brotherhood.[^cite_note-Mauritania_Carnegie-289]

In 2007 the [National Rally for Reform and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rally_for_Reform_and_Development), better known as Tewassoul, was legalized as a political party. The party is associated with the Mauritanian branch of the Muslim Brotherhood.[^cite_note-Mauritania_Carnegie-289]

#### Morocco

Further information: [Justice and Development Party (Morocco)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Development_Party_\(Morocco\))

The [Justice and Development Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Development_Party_\(Morocco\)) was the largest vote-getter in [Morocco's 2011 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Morocco#Political_parties_and_elections), and as of May 2015, held the office of Prime Minister.[^cite_note-Ibish-40] It is historically affiliated with the Muslim Brotherhood,[^cite_note-menas-290][^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293] however, despite this, PJD was never an official branch[^cite_note-menas-290] and the party has reportedly "ostentatiously" praised the King of Morocco, while "loudly insisting that it is in no sense whatsoever a Muslim Brotherhood party".[^cite_note-Ibish-40]

#### Somalia

Al-Islah has been described as "a generally nonviolent and modernizing Islamic movement that emphasizes the reformation and revival of Islam to meet the challenges of the modern world", whose "goal is the establishment of an [Islamic state](2646438.md)" and which "operates primarily in Mogadishu".[^cite_note-state2004-294]

#### Sudan

Further information: [National Islamic Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Islamic_Front), [National Congress Party (Sudan)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Congress_Party_\(Sudan\)), and [Islamism in Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamism_in_Sudan)

An offshoot of the Sudanese branch of the Muslim Brotherhood, the [Islamic Charter Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Islamic_Front) grew during the 1960, with Islamic scholar [Hasan al-Turabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_al-Turabi) becoming its secretary general in 1964.[^cite_note-Natsios-84-295] The Islamic Charter Front (ICM) was renamed several times most recently being called the [National Islamic Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Islamic_Front) (NIF). The Muslim Brotherhood/NIF's main objective in Sudan was to Islamize the society "from above" and to institutionalize the Islamic law throughout the country where they succeeded. To that end the party infiltrated the top echelons of the government where the education of party cadre, frequently acquired in the West, made them "indispensable". This approach was described by Turabi himself as the "jurisprudence of necessity".[^cite_note-kepel-179-296]

Meeting resistance from non-Islamists, from already established Muslim organisations, and from non-Muslims in the south, the Sudanese NIF government under Turabi and the NIF organized a coup to overthrow a democratically elected government in 1989, organized the Popular Defense Force which committed "widespread, deliberate and systematic atrocities against hundreds of thousands of southern civilians" in the 1990s.[^cite_note-Natsios-85-297] The NIF government also employed "widespread arbitrary and extrajudicial arrest, torture, and execution of labor union officials, military officers, journalists, political figures and civil society leaders".[^cite_note-Natsios-85-297]

The NCP was dissolved in the aftermath of the [military takeover](60469131.md) on 11 April 2019.[^cite_note-298]

#### Tunisia

Further information: [Ennahda Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ennahda_Movement)

### Europe

Muslim Brotherhood organisations in Europe find themselves in different circumstances compared to their counterparts in the Muslim World, as they in Europe operate in societies which do not have a Muslim majority. The first Brotherhood members active in Europe migrated from the Middle East during the late 1950s and early 1960s. Some were in the leadership of the Egyptian Brotherhood who fled the dictatorship of [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser). Most were foreign students who already had Brotherhood sympathies while others were experienced militants. Together they continued their Islamic activities in the destination countries, where Europe's freedoms allowed them to openly conduct activities which had been banned in the Muslim countries of origin.[^cite_note-\:2-299]

Student groups affiliated to the MB grew into organizations and they often structured their mosques as community centres. Following al-Banna's organizational model they founded women's groups, think tanks and schools. This growth was funded by both public and private donors in the [Arab gulf countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_gulf_countries).[^cite_note-\:2-299]

By establishing a multitude of organizations devoted to anything from education, financial investments, political lobbying and charity they supported the growing Muslim populations of Europe and sought to shape the direction of [Islam in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Europe). Thereby the brothers created a *de facto* branch of the Muslim Brotherhood in every European country. To increase their potential for gaining influence, the organisations established by the MB are often given names which portray a facade of broad representation (e.g. "Muslim association of European Country") or religious moderation (e.g. "Islamic Co-Existence Alliance"). This is in contrast to the views propagated by many speakers at MB events where the Western countries are condemned as being corrupt, unjust and immoral along with a narrative where Muslims are portrayed as better, but beleaguered. On certain issues such as religious freedom, women's rights and homosexuality Brotherhood spokespersons espouse ideas contrary to mainstream European values and basic human rights.[^cite_note-\:2-299]

The pan-European umbrella organization of the Brotherhood in Europe is the [Federation of Islamic Organizations in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Islamic_Organizations_in_Europe) which was founded in 1989 and in 2020 changed its name to Council of European Muslims. It has its headquarters a few blocks away from key European Union institutions in Brussels.[^cite_note-\:2-299]

#### France

The brotherhood's build-up in France started with *Union des organisations islamiques en France* (UOIF) which later changed its name to [Musulmans de France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslims_of_France_\(organization\)). The organization primarily consisted of foreign students who entered France from Tunisia and Morocco. By 2020, there were 147 mosques and 18 Islamic schools associated with the brotherhood. UOIF has about 50,000 members distributed among 200 member organizations.[^cite_note-\:1-300]

The Brotherhood pursues a [communitarianist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communitarianism) philiosophy and works against Muslims adopting liberal lifestyles and becoming assimilated into French society. In the long term, they aim towards entering politics by increasing the number of Muslims until they can form a political party of their own.[^cite_note-\:1-300]

In January 2026, the [French National Assembly](299616.md) adopted a non-binding resolution calling on the [European Union](9317.md) to assess whether the Muslim Brotherhood should be added to the EU's list of terrorist organisations. The resolution urged EU institutions to examine the movement's activities and networks in Europe, citing concerns about its ideological influence and alleged links to extremist currents. According to various sources, the vote reflected growing scrutiny in France of the Muslim Brotherhood's role in civil society and its perceived challenge to secular and republican values, while government officials emphasised that the measure did not amount to an immediate designation and that any EU-level decision would require a legal assessment and consensus among member states.[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302]

#### Germany

The [Islamic Community of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Community_of_Germany) *(de: Islamische Gemeinschaft in Deutschland e.V, IGD)* being constituent and founding organisation of the MB umbrella organisation FIOE, the MB is active in Germany with the IGD as a proxy. IGD members take care to not publicly declare their affiliation to the MB.[^cite_note-BSTMI_2012_p3435-303]

#### Netherlands

In March 2026, a majority of members of the [Dutch House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_House_of_Representatives) supported a motion calling for a ban on the Muslim Brotherhood and affiliated organisations. The motion was backed by 76 MPs, although some parties, including the [CDA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democratic_Appeal), argued that such a ban would be legally difficult because the Muslim Brotherhood is not a formally structured organisation in the Netherlands.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305]

#### Russia

The Muslim Brotherhood is banned in Russia as a [terrorist organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorist_organisation).[^cite_note-RF_SC_Decision-306]

As affirmed on 14 February 2003 by the decision of the [Supreme Court of Russia](5915064.md), the Muslim Brotherhood coordinated the creation of an Islamic organisation called *[The Supreme Military Majlis ul-Shura of the United Forces of Caucasian Mujahedeen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Supreme_Military_Majlis_ul-Shura_of_the_United_Forces_of_Caucasian_Mujahedeen?action=edit\&redlink=1)* ([Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language): Высший военный маджлисуль шура объединённых сил моджахедов Кавказа), led by [Ibn Al-Khattab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Al-Khattab) and [Basaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basaev); an organisation that committed multiple terror-attack acts in Russia and was allegedly financed by drug trafficking, counterfeiting of coins and racketeering.[^cite_note-RF_SC_Decision-306]

#### United Kingdom

The Muslim Brotherhood uses [London](17867.md) as an administration base.[^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308]

The first Muslim Brotherhood-affiliated organisations in the UK were founded in the 1960s, which comprised exiles and overseas students.[^cite_note-\:0-309] They promoted the works of Indian theologician [Abu A'la Mawdudi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abul_A'la_Maududi) and represented the [Jama'at-e-Islami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamaat-e-Islami). In their initial phase they were politically inactive in the UK as they assumed they would return to their home countries and instead focused on recruiting new members and to support the MB in the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world).[^cite_note-\:0-309]

In the late 1980s and early 1990s, the MB and its associated organisations changed to a new strategy of political activity in western countries with the purpose to promote the MB overseas but also preserve the autonomy of Muslim communities in the UK.[^cite_note-\:0-309]

In the 1990s, the MB established publicly visible organisations and ostensibly "national" organisations to further its agenda, but membership in the MB was and remains a secret.[^cite_note-\:0-309] The MB dominated the [Islamic Society of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Society_of_Britain) (ISB), the [Muslim Association of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_Association_of_Britain) (MAB) and founded the [Muslim Council of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_Council_of_Britain) (MCB). MAB became politically active in foreign policy issues such as Palestine and Iraq, while MCB established a dialogue with the then governments.[^cite_note-\:0-309]

Since 2001, the ISB has distanced itself from Muslim Brotherhood ideology along with the MCB.[^cite_note-\:0-309]

In April 2014, [David Cameron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Cameron), who was the [prime minister of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_minister_of_the_United_Kingdom) at the time, launched an investigation into the Muslim Brotherhood's activities in the UK and its alleged extremist activities.[^cite_note-DavidCameron-310]

In a 2015 government report, the MB was found to not have been linked to terrorist related activity against in the UK and MAB has condemned [Al-Qaeda](1921.md) terrorist activity in the UK.[^cite_note-\:0-309]

According to later investigations by *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)*, this report was the result of pressure from the United Arab Emirates. Starting in 2012, the Emirates engaged in targeted lobbying of the British government to push it to take action against the Muslim Brotherhood. In return, the Emirates held out the prospect of investments worth billions. During a conversation with the head of the inquiry commission, a representative of the UAE warned: 'If Britain does not take its allies into account, the difficult discussions we are having will become even more difficult. We are sending a warning signal.[^cite_note-311]

### Other states

#### Australia

The Muslim Brotherhood Movement is an [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Australians) [street gang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street_gang) in [Sydney](27862.md) that uses the same name as the Muslim Brotherhood.[^cite_note-312]

In 2013, members of Sydney's [Egyptian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Australians) community reported that the Muslim Brotherhood had opened an office in [Western Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Sydney), following the election and later overthrowing of Egyptian president Mursi, both of which caused protests in Sydney.[^cite_note-313]

#### Indonesia

Further information: [Prosperous Justice Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosperous_Justice_Party)

Several parties and organizations in Indonesia are linked or at least inspired by the Muslim Brotherhood, although none have a formal relationship with the Muslim Brotherhood. One of the Muslim Brotherhood-linked parties is the [PKS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosperous_Justice_Party) (Prosperous Justice Party),[^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315] which gained 6.79% of votes in the [2014 legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Indonesian_legislative_election), down from 7.88% in the [2009 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Indonesian_legislative_election). The PKS's relationship with the Egyptian Muslim Brotherhood was confirmed by [Yusuf al-Qaradawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_al-Qaradawi), a prominent Muslim Brotherhood leader.[^cite_note-316]

#### Malaysia

The [Malaysian Islamic Party (PAS)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Islamic_Party_\(PAS\)), the oldest and largest mainstream Islamist party in Malaysia, has close personal and ideological ties with the Egyptian Muslim Brotherhood.[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318] Founded in 1951, PAS's founders were exposed to the ideas and teachings while they were studying in Cairo during the 1940s. PAS was the main rival to the Malay nationalist [United Malays National Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Malays_National_Organisation), which dominated Malaysian politics until 2018. Due to changes in political situation created by Pakatan Harapan (PH)'s win in 2018 election, PAS has made [a cooperation pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muafakat_Nasional) with UMNO in 2019.[^cite_note-319] Together with a former PH component party (BERSATU) both parties ultimately took over [the government](9660028.md) during [2020–21 Malaysian political crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%9321_Malaysian_political_crisis).[^cite_note-320] According to the think tank [Institute for Democracy and Economic Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_Democracy_and_Economic_Affairs)' CEO Wan Saiful Wan Jan, PAS is regarded by the Muslim Brotherhood as an electorally successful Islamic political party; PAS has governed the state of [Kelantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelantan) since 2002. PAS representatives are often invited to Muslim Brotherhood speaking engagements overseas. In 2012, PAS president [Abdul Hadi Awang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Hadi_Awang) spoke alongside Muslim Brotherhood scholar Sheikh [Yusuf al-Qaradawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_al-Qaradawi) at a speaking event in London.[^cite_note-321] In April 2014, PAS leader Abdul Awang spoke out against Saudi Arabia, Bahrain, and the United Arab Emirates' decision to designate the Muslim Brotherhood as a terrorist organization.[^cite_note-322]

According to Bubalo and Fealy, *[Angkatan Belia Islam Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angkatan_Belia_Islam_Malaysia)* (or the Muslim Youth Movement of Malaysia) was inspired or influenced by the Muslim Brotherhood.[^cite_note-323]

#### New Zealand

In 2016, [Papanui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papanui) ward candidate John Stringer alleged that a Muslim Brotherhood cell was active in northwestern [Christchurch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christchurch).[^cite_note-324]

#### United States

According to a 2004 article by *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*, U.S. Muslim Brotherhood supporters "make up the U.S. Islamic community's most organized force" by running hundreds of mosques and business ventures, promoting civic activities, and setting up American Islamic organizations to defend and promote Islam.[^cite_note-325] In 1963, the U.S. chapter of Muslim Brotherhood was started by activists involved with the [Muslim Students Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_Students_Association) (MSA).[^cite_note-WashingtonPost-Diverse-245] U.S. supporters of the Brotherhood also started other organizations including: [North American Islamic Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Islamic_Trust) in 1971, the [Islamic Society of North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Society_of_North_America) in 1981, the [American Muslim Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Muslim_Council) in 1990, the [Muslim American Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_American_Society) in 1992 and the [International Institute of Islamic Thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_of_Islamic_Thought) in the 1980s.[^cite_note-WashingtonPost-Diverse-245] In addition, according to *An Explanatory Memorandum on the General Strategic Goal for the Group in North America*, the "Understanding of the Role of the Muslim Brotherhood in North America", and a relatively benign goal of the Muslim Brotherhood in North America is identified as the following:

> Establishing an effective and a stable Islamic movement led by the Muslim Brotherhood which adopts Muslims' causes domestically and globally, and which works to expand the observant Muslim base, aims at unifying and directing Muslims' efforts, presents Islam as a civilization alternative, and supports the global Islamic state wherever it is.[^cite_note-q\:Muslim_Brotherhood-326][^cite_note-txnd-327]

> The process of settlement is a 'Civilization-Jihadist Process' with all the word means. The Ikhwan \[Muslim Brotherhood] must understand that their work in America is a kind of grand jihad in eliminating and destroying the Western civilization from within and 'sabotaging' its miserable house by their hands and the hands of the believers so that it is eliminated and God's religion \[Islam] is made victorious over all other religions.[^cite_note-q\:Muslim_Brotherhood-326][^cite_note-txnd-327]

During the [Holy Land Foundation trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Land_Foundation_for_Relief_and_Development#Terrorism_charges) in 2007, several documents pertaining to the Brotherhood were unsuccessful in convincing the courts that the Brotherhood was involved in subversive activities. In one, dated 1984 called "Ikhwan in America" (Brotherhood in America), the author alleges that the activities of the Muslim Brotherhood in the US include going to camps to do weapons training (referred to as *special work* by the Muslim Brotherhood),[^cite_note-NEFAFoundation-328] as well as engaging in [counter-espionage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-espionage) against U.S. government agencies such as the [FBI](11127.md) and [CIA](5183633.md) (referred to as *Securing the Group*).[^cite_note-NEFAFoundation-328] Another (dated 1991) outlined a strategy for the Muslim Brotherhood in the United States that involved "eliminating and destroying the Western civilization from within".[^cite_note-nyt2011-07-31-329][^cite_note-330][^cite_note-txnd-327]

The documents continue to be widely publicized in American conservative circles.[^cite_note-nyt2011-07-31-329]

U.S. Congress attempts to pass legislation criminalizing the group, put forward by the 114th Congress, were defeated. The Bill, called the Muslim Brotherhood Terrorist Designation Act of 2015, was introduced to the [Senate Committee on Foreign Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_Foreign_Relations) by Senator [Ted Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Cruz) (R-TX). In it the bill states that the [Department of State](31975.md) should designate the Muslim Brotherhood as a terrorist organization. If passed, the bill would have required the State Department to report to Congress within 60 days whether or not the group fits the criteria, and if it did not, to state which specific criteria it had not met.[^cite_note-331]

This bill came after a handful of foreign countries made similar moves in recent years including Egypt, Russia, Saudi Arabia, and others, and after, according to Cruz, recent evidence emerged suggesting that the group supports terrorism. The senator further alleged that the group's stated goal is to wage violent jihad against its enemies, which includes the United States, and the fact that the Obama administration has listed numerous group members on its terror list. Cruz further stated that the bill would "reject the fantasy that \[the] parent institution \[of the Muslim Brotherhood] is a political entity that is somehow separate from these violent activities".[^cite_note-Hattem-332]

The bill identifies three Muslim Brotherhood entities in the U.S. including the [Council on American–Islamic Relations](159138.md) (CAIR), a non-profit group denounced by the UAE for its MB ties. The other two entities are the [Islamic Society of North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Society_of_North_America) (ISNA) and the [North American Islamic Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Islamic_Trust) (NAIT).[^cite_note-counterjihadreport.com-333]

Conservatives in the Congress believe that the group is a breeding ground for radical Islam. Previous attempts were made in the previous year by Representative [Michele Bachmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michele_Bachmann) (R-MN), but it failed largely due to her allegation that [Huma Abedin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huma_Abedin), [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton)'s aide, had links to the organization, a statement which was dismissed by establishment Democrats and Republicans.[^cite_note-Hattem-332]

In February 2016, the [House Judiciary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Committee_on_the_Judiciary) approved the legislation in a 17 to 10 vote, which if enacted could increase grounds for enforcing criminal penalties and give permission to the [Secretary of Treasury](43338.md) to block financial transactions and freeze assets of anyone who has showed material support for the group. Scholars against this classification claim that the group simply promotes Islamism, or the belief that society should be governed according to Islamic values and Sharia law.[^cite_note-334]

Past U.S. presidential administrations have examined whether to designate the Muslim Brotherhood as a Foreign Terrorist Organization and have decided not to do so.[^cite_note-335] During the [George W. Bush](3414021.md) administration, the U.S. government investigated the Brotherhood and associated Islamist groups, but "after years of investigations, ... the U.S. and other governments, including Switzerland's, closed investigations of the Brotherhood leaders and financial group for lack of evidence, and removed most of the leaders from sanctions lists."[^cite_note-336] The Obama administration was also pressured to designate the Brotherhood as a terrorist organization, but did not do so.[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-nyt-Baker-338] During the first [Donald Trump](4848272.md) administration, there were serious steps towards designating the Muslim Brotherhood as a terrorist organization.[^cite_note-339]

In November 2025 a new report was presented to members of Congress, senior diplomats, policy experts and leading scholars in a [Washington](108956.md) event. According to [ISGAP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_the_Study_of_Global_Antisemitism_and_Policy), the Muslim Brotherhood is operating by a long, 100-year plan intended to spread its influence inside Western governments, schools, and other institutions, while holding beliefs that go against democratic values. The reports that is based on internal documents and network studies, shows clear connections, ideas, organization, and money transfers between the Brotherhood, Hamas, and other groups. The report also shows that Qatar is one of the organization's main supporters.[^cite_note-340]
